---
title: 6. k-NN & naive Bayes
description: k-nearest neighbours (distance metrics, choice of k, scaling, curse of dimensionality) and the naive Bayes classifier (Bayes rule, conditional independence, parameter counting, Laplace smoothing, misclassification probability).
sidebar:
  label: 6. k-NN & naive Bayes
  order: 6
---

**Why it matters:** 2024 Q63 (smallest odd $k$ that flips a label), 2024 Q20 (how many parameters naive Bayes needs) and 2025 Q35 (probability that naive Bayes misclassifies) come from this page. These are two of the simplest classifiers, and GATE tests them **numerically**.

## Topic map

<div class="mindmap">

- **k-NN & naive Bayes**
  - k-NN
    - store everything; vote among the $k$ closest
    - distances: Euclidean, Manhattan, Minkowski
    - small $k$ → high variance; large $k$ → high bias
    - scale the features; curse of dimensionality
    - k-NN regression = average of the neighbours
  - Bayes classifier
    - pick $\arg\max_y P(y)P(x \mid y)$
    - error $= 1 - \max_y P(y \mid x)$
  - Naive Bayes
    - features independent **given the class**
    - parameter count $\approx$ linear in the number of features
    - Bernoulli / multinomial / Gaussian versions
    - Laplace smoothing, log probabilities

</div>

## 6.1 k-nearest neighbours: the idea

To classify a new point, find the $k$ **training points closest to it** and take a **majority vote** of their labels. For regression, average their target values instead.

There's no training phase: the "model" is the stored training set. That's why k-NN is called a **lazy**, **instance-based**, **non-parametric** learner.

<div class="formula">

**Distances** between $x, z \in \mathbb{R}^d$:

$$
\text{Euclidean } \|x - z\|_2 = \sqrt{\textstyle\sum_j (x_j - z_j)^2} \qquad
\text{Manhattan } \|x - z\|_1 = \textstyle\sum_j |x_j - z_j| \qquad
\text{Minkowski } \Big(\textstyle\sum_j |x_j - z_j|^p\Big)^{1/p}
$$

Minkowski with $p = 1$ is Manhattan, $p = 2$ is Euclidean, and $p \to \infty$ gives $\max_j |x_j - z_j|$.

**Cost:** training $O(1)$; predicting one point $O(nd)$, since you compute the distance to every training point.

</div>

**Working a k-NN question:**

1. List the distance from the query to **every** training point. Use squared distances for Euclidean if you like; the ordering doesn't change.
2. Sort them.
3. Vote among the first $k$. Watch for **ties in distance** at the $k$th place.

width="6.8" height="6.8"/><rect class="grid" x="320" y="30" width="270" height="220"/></svg><figcaption>The same 60 noisy training points classified by 1-NN and 15-NN. With k = 1 every point gets its own island, so the boundary is jagged (low bias, high variance; training error is 0). With k = 15 the boundary is smooth (higher bias, lower variance).</figcaption></figure>
<!-- /fig:knn-k-effect -->

| $k$ | Boundary | Bias | Variance | Training error |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | jagged; each point owns its Voronoi cell | low | **high** | **0** (each point is its own neighbour) |
| moderate | smoother | ↑ | ↓ | > 0 |
| $k = n$ | constant: always the majority class | **high** | low | that of the majority-class rule |

- Use an **odd $k$** for two classes to avoid tied votes.
- Choose $k$ by **cross-validation**.
- **Weighted k-NN** gives closer neighbours more say (for example, weight $\propto 1/\text{distance}$).

## 6.3 Two practical warnings

**Scale the features.** Distances add up across features, so a feature measured in rupees (range 0–1,00,000) drowns out one measured in years (range 0–50). Standardise first, using z-scores or min-max scaling (DBMS → data transformation).

**The curse of dimensionality.** In high dimensions, distances between random points become **nearly equal**. The nearest neighbour is barely nearer than the farthest, so "nearest" stops meaning "similar". Covering the space densely also needs exponentially many points as $d$ grows. k-NN works best in low dimensions, or after dimensionality reduction (PCA, topic 12).

## 6.4 The Bayes classifier

Suppose we knew the true probabilities. The decision that minimises the error rate is to pick the class with the largest **posterior**:

$$
\hat y = \arg\max_y P(y \mid x) = \arg\max_y \frac{P(x \mid y)\,P(y)}{P(x)} = \arg\max_y P(x \mid y)\,P(y).
$$

$P(x)$ is the same for every class, so it can be dropped when you only need the **argmax**. But you need it to get the actual posterior **values**.

<div class="formula">

**Probability of misclassifying a given $x$** $= 1 - \max_y P(y \mid x)$. You're wrong exactly when the true class is one of the others.

To get the posteriors, normalise the scores: $P(y_k \mid x) = \dfrac{P(x \mid y_k)P(y_k)}{\sum_j P(x \mid y_j)P(y_j)}$.

</div>

## 6.5 Naive Bayes

**The problem.** With $K$ binary features, $P(x \mid y)$ is a table over $2^K$ feature combinations for each class. That's far too many parameters to estimate from limited data.

**The naive assumption.** The features are **conditionally independent given the class**:

$$
P(x_1, \dots, x_K \mid y) = \prod_{j=1}^K P(x_j \mid y).
$$

Now each feature needs only its own small table per class.

<div class="formula">

**Parameter count: $K$ binary features, 2 classes**

| Model | Parameters |
|---|---|
| Prior $P(y = 1)$ | 1 |
| Naive Bayes: $P(x_j = 1 \mid y)$ for each $j$ and each class | $2K$ |
| **Naive Bayes total** | $\mathbf{2K + 1}$ |
| Full joint $P(x \mid y)$, no independence | $2(2^K - 1) + 1$ |

In general, with $C$ classes and $K$ features each taking $v$ values: $(C - 1) + C\,K\,(v - 1)$.
**Gaussian NB** with $d$ real features: a mean and a variance per feature per class, $2Cd$, plus $C - 1$ priors.

</div>

The independence assumption is usually **false** (words in an email aren't independent), but naive Bayes still classifies surprisingly well. You only need the **argmax** to come out right, not the exact probabilities.

**Naive Bayes is generative.** It models $P(x \mid y)$ and $P(y)$ (topic 1).

### Worked example

Ten emails, with features *offer* $\in \{0, 1\}$ and *meeting* $\in \{0, 1\}$:

| Class | Count | $P(\text{class})$ | $P(\text{offer} = 1 \mid \text{class})$ | $P(\text{meeting} = 1 \mid \text{class})$ |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Spam | 4 | 0.4 | 3/4 | 1/4 |
| Ham | 6 | 0.6 | 1/6 | 4/6 |

Classify a new email with offer = 1 and meeting = 0:

- Spam score $= 0.4 \times \frac34 \times (1 - \frac14) = 0.225$
- Ham score $= 0.6 \times \frac16 \times (1 - \frac46) = 0.0333$
- $P(\text{spam} \mid x) = \frac{0.225}{0.225 + 0.0333} = 0.871$, so predict **spam**. The probability of misclassification is $0.129$.

Note the factor $(1 - p)$ for a feature that is **absent**. Bernoulli naive Bayes counts absent features too.

### Zero counts and Laplace smoothing

If a word never appears in spam training emails, $P(\text{word} \mid \text{spam}) = 0$, and that single zero wipes out the whole product. **Laplace (add-one) smoothing** fixes this:

$$
P(x_j = v \mid y) = \frac{\text{count}(x_j = v, y) + 1}{\text{count}(y) + V},
$$

where $V$ is the number of values $x_j$ can take (2 for binary features, or the vocabulary size for word counts in multinomial NB).

**Underflow:** multiplying hundreds of small probabilities underflows to 0 on a computer, so in practice we add **log probabilities** instead. The argmax is unchanged.

### Variants

| Variant | Feature type | $P(x_j \mid y)$ |
|---|---|---|
| Bernoulli NB | binary (present / absent) | $p_{jy}^{x_j}(1 - p_{jy})^{1 - x_j}$ |
| Multinomial NB | counts (word frequencies) | proportional to $\theta_{jy}^{\,x_j}$ |
| Gaussian NB | real-valued | $\mathcal{N}(x_j;\, \mu_{jy}, \sigma^2_{jy})$ |

---

## Summary: important points

<div class="formula">

1. k-NN: compute every distance, sort, and vote among the $k$ nearest. It's lazy and non-parametric, with $O(nd)$ prediction.
2. Small $k$ means low bias and high variance (1-NN has zero training error). Large $k$ means the opposite. Use odd $k$ for two classes.
3. Always scale the features. k-NN suffers from the curse of dimensionality.
4. The Bayes rule picks $\arg\max_y P(x \mid y)P(y)$. Misclassification probability $= 1 - \max$ posterior.
5. Naive Bayes assumes the features are independent **given the class**.
6. $K$ binary features with 2 classes need $2K + 1$ parameters.
7. Laplace smoothing: $(\text{count} + 1)/(N_y + V)$. Use log probabilities to avoid underflow.
8. Naive Bayes is generative. k-NN is discriminative and non-parametric.

</div>

<div class="trap">

- Naive Bayes assumes **conditional** independence (given $y$), not that the features are independent overall.
- To get the posterior, divide by the **sum** of the class scores. The raw product isn't a probability.
- In Bernoulli NB, an absent feature contributes $1 - p$, not 1.
- In k-NN, check for a distance tie at the $k$th neighbour (2024 Q63 has one at $\sqrt2$).

</div>

---

## Test yourself (A): previous-year questions

### GATE DA 2024 Q63 (2 marks, NAT)

Circles at (1, 1) and (1, 2), squares at (2, 3), (3, 3) and (3, 2), Euclidean distance. What is the smallest odd $k$ for which k-NN labels the query (2, 1) as square?

<details>
<summary>Show solution</summary>

**Answer: 5**

Distances from (2, 1):

| Point | Class | Distance |
|---|---|---|
| (1, 1) | ● | 1 |
| (1, 2) | ● | $\sqrt2$ |
| (3, 2) | ■ | $\sqrt2$ |
| (2, 3) | ■ | 2 |
| (3, 3) | ■ | $\sqrt5$ |

- $k = 1$: ● (circle).
- $k = 3$: ●, ●, ■, so circle wins 2–1. The tie at $\sqrt2$ doesn't matter, because both tied points fit inside the 3 neighbours.
- $k = 5$: 2 ● vs 3 ■, so **square**.

</details>

### GATE DA 2024 Q20 (1 mark, MCQ)

How many parameters must be estimated to learn naive Bayes for a two-class task with $K$ binary attributes ($K > 2$)?

<details>
<summary>Show solution</summary>

**Answer: $2K + 1$**

- One prior, $P(y = 1)$. $P(y = 0)$ is $1 - P(y = 1)$.
- For each attribute $j$ and each of the 2 classes, $P(x_j = 1 \mid y)$. That's $2K$ numbers.

Total: $2K + 1$. Without the naive assumption it would be $2(2^K - 1) + 1$.

</details>

### GATE DA 2025 Q35 (1 mark, NAT)

Naive Bayes with priors $P(y_1) = 1/3$ and $P(y_2) = 2/3$. For a feature vector $x$, $P(x \mid y_1) = 3/4$ and $P(x \mid y_2) = 1/4$. What is the probability of misclassifying $x$? (2 decimal places)

<details>
<summary>Show solution</summary>

**Answer: 0.40**

- Scores: $y_1$: $\frac13 \times \frac34 = \frac14$. $y_2$: $\frac23 \times \frac14 = \frac16$.
- Posteriors: $P(y_1 \mid x) = \frac{1/4}{1/4 + 1/6} = \frac{3}{5} = 0.6$ and $P(y_2 \mid x) = 0.4$.
- The classifier picks $y_1$. It's wrong whenever the true class is $y_2$: probability **0.40**.

</details>

---

## Test yourself (B): predicted 2027-style questions

**F1 (1 mark, MCQ).** Training points are A: (1,1), (3,3), (2,2) and B: (2,4), (4,2), (5,5). Using **Manhattan** distance and $k = 3$, what is the label of the query (3, 2)?

<details>
<summary>Show solution</summary>

**Answer: A**

Manhattan distances from (3, 2):

- (2, 2): 1 (A); (3, 3): 1 (A); (4, 2): 1 (B)
- (1, 1): 3 (A); (2, 4): 3 (B); (5, 5): 5 (B)

The three nearest are A, A, B, so the vote goes to **A**.

</details>

**F2 (1 mark, NAT).** k-NN regression with $k = 3$. The three nearest neighbours have targets 10, 14 and 18. What is the prediction?

<details>
<summary>Show solution</summary>

**Answer: 14**

The mean of the neighbours' targets is $(10 + 14 + 18)/3 = $ **14**.

</details>

**F3 (2 marks, NAT).** In the email example of §6.5, what is $P(\text{spam} \mid \text{offer} = 0, \text{meeting} = 1)$? (2 decimal places)

<details>
<summary>Show solution</summary>

**Answer: 0.07**

- Spam: $0.4 \times \frac14 \times \frac14 = 0.025$ (offer absent: $1 - \frac34$; meeting present: $\frac14$).
- Ham: $0.6 \times \frac56 \times \frac46 = 0.3333$.
- Posterior: $\frac{0.025}{0.025 + 0.3333} = 0.0698 \approx$ **0.07**.

</details>

**F4 (1 mark, NAT).** A word appears 0 times in the 20 training documents of a class. The vocabulary has 10 words. Using add-one smoothing with denominator $\text{count}(y) + V$, what is the smoothed estimate? (3 decimal places)

<details>
<summary>Show solution</summary>

**Answer: 0.033**

$(0 + 1)/(20 + 10) = 1/30 = $ **0.033**.

</details>

**F5 (1 mark, NAT).** How many parameters does Gaussian naive Bayes need for 3 classes and 4 real-valued features (means, variances and priors)?

<details>
<summary>Show solution</summary>

**Answer: 26**

$3 \times 4 \times 2 = 24$ means and variances, plus $3 - 1 = 2$ free priors. Total **26**.

</details>

**F6 (2 marks, MSQ).** Which statements about k-NN are true?
(A) 1-NN always has zero training error (when there are no duplicate points with different labels) (B) Increasing $k$ generally increases bias (C) k-NN needs a long training phase to fit its parameters (D) Without feature scaling, features with a large range dominate the distance

<details>
<summary>Show solution</summary>

**Answer: A; B; D**

(C) is false: k-NN is lazy. Its "training" is just storing the data.

</details>
